ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e117	Honourable co-representatives! Former depositor Martin Johannes Toralf Buvik died on February 17th, 95 years old. With Martin Buvik's death, the Right has lost a chieftain of format and North Norway is one of the country's most insidious politicians in the post-war period. Buvik represented Right on the Stortinget from Troms County for three periods, from 1965 to 1977. As a representative, he was the first member of the Church and Education Committee and the last two members of the Municipal and Environmental Protection Committee. He was then County in Troms from 1977 to 1990. With his wide-ranging knowledge and moody style, he was in demand as a leader and a member of public authority. He led, among other things: two municipal zoning committees, the so-called Buvik committees. He was director of the Oil Directorate from 1977 to 1989, head of the Rein driftstyreldet from 1980 to 1987, was a member of the Defence Commission of 1990 and was also head of the public body to assess civil protection. For many years he was a member and later chairman of the Finnish-Norwegian Cultural Fund. Among other things, for his commitment to Finnish-Norwegian cooperation, he was appointed Commander of the Order of the Vita Ros of Finland in 1985. He also received King Olav V's 25th anniversary medal in 1982. Martin Buvik was born and grew up in former Trondenes, later the municipality of Skånland. He graduated from high school in 1939, but the war put an end to further education until he could take the graduation artium as a privateist in 1945. He showed early pedagogical abilities. As a 19-year-old, he was an established teacher in the primary school in Skånland, and he worked there and in Evenes as a teacher from 1942 to 1947. Later, there was a study stay in England. In the right he started his career as District Secretary of the Young Right Troms, 1948 to 1950, and was county secretary in Troms Right from 1950 to 1965. He was a member of the Municipality of Tromsøysund from 1956 to 1963 and of the City of Tromsø from 1964 to 1967. He gained important trust for the Right Central, among others. as Chairman of the Committee of the Higher Education Committee from 1966 to 1970 and as Chairman of the Committee of the National Commissions of the Court of Justice from 1972 to 1977. Trom's Right appointed him honorary in 1997. Martin Buvik was an inspiration for many and willingly contributed with good advice and experience. Mild in form and strong in content, always positive and with glimpses in the eye. Until recently, he set up political meetings, took the floor and shared knowledge, analysis and opinions. It tells a lot about his strong and long community exposure. We bring peace to Martin Buvik's memory.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e217	I agree with what the rapporteur said about the big message of cooperation in the Nordic region, which the Stortinget now has to deal with. This communication gives us a good overview of the scope and scope of cooperation that in many areas must be considered to be very unique in the international context. There is close contact, dialogue and willingness to adapt across legislation and borders through 65 years that has laid the foundation for the Integrated Nordic Region, as we experience today. An important prerequisite for the emergence and interest in further developing Nordic cooperation is that it is perceived as being current, that it is widely welcomed, and that it addresses issues of which its citizens are concerned. We shall all be pleased to note that the emergence of Nordic cooperation in our peoples is increasing and very clear. We will be glad to discuss this matter in Stortinget today. What we call the ‘Nordic model’ is unique in the world – with a relatively high level of taxation, good state benefits in the event of sickness, free education and a good public pension. This is an important part of what is called the Nordic model, and I believe that this is a prerequisite for strong popular support for Nordic cooperation. The Nordic model is characterised by trust between the rulers and the governed and between employees and employers, by economic developments with a bearing on the interests of the community and by the inclusion of women and gender equality. This testifies to a common basis of values for the people of the Nordic region. The model and cooperation are also based on strong democracy and popular rule. It may be statesmen who protest at what I am now saying, but I would nevertheless like to say that the Nordic countries and the Nordic model are so strong and have such strong popular support, linked to the fact that the Nordic countries – more than other countries – are strongly popularly overthrown, that democracy is strong and governing. The strong rule of law follows, of course, in the extension of this, relatively small differences, a large public sector, but above all humanism – that in the Nordic countries we are able to view each other as equivalent citizens, and that we live in a humane and humane society. The cooperation in the Nordic Council, of which I am now a part, bears the mark of the desire to care and build on this fine foundation. The North is strong in the world because we have these values, and because there is a balance in society. On that basis, we have gained more than many other countries have done. Nordic society is strong and has a high authority in the international context. That is why it is important to be told that we respond with joy — sometimes also with wonder — when colleagues from other countries come to us to see and learn, as they say. They also sometimes ask – at least I have heard it quite many times – about how we have got to this. That's often a question. I believe that the answer to that question is what I have already said: that we have a strong rule of people, and that we view each other as equivalent people. The North and the unique cooperation are successful for humans, but not just for them. It's also a successful economic model. The balance of society, as described, creates good opportunities for stable profits and returns. I would say, with a little smile on my mouth, that there has actually been a successful capitalism in the Nordic countries because of these conditions. This is an important recognition that comes from other countries, and that is why you are also applying for it here. Those who are obviously interested in making money from their money are also coming to the North for this very reason: there are stable communities that can provide stable returns. This gives international attention and is referred to in the international economic environments with great attention and emphasis. But – and I've said this before, but I want to repeat it – despite everything that we really agree on in this room when we talk about Nordic cooperation, it's something that, more than other things, is the bearer's beam, and that it's very important to be told by all the crossroads that it doesn't have to be challenged. I've said the words – that we view each other as equivalent. By extension, I would say that the right to apply for asylum in the Nordic countries must be real. Of course, we're going to have safe borders; those who don't get to stay are going back, but we're not going to have to talk down about those who are going to be here. It has to do with the cultural and social content of the Nordic model. Nor must we – within the Nordic sphere – challenge the legal certainty of these people. These are crucial questions. This will determine whether the Nordic model carries for the future. The Nordic countries and Norway are also dependent on these people. It's said too rarely, and that's why I want to focus on it. We're actually dependent on these people. Many of these people are doing a job in the Nordic communities that it would have been almost impossible for us to do, if they had not been present in the Nordic communities. That is why it is also necessary to protect them on this basis, and it is necessary to welcome them. This is being challenged at regular intervals, including in the Nordic parliaments and in the Nordic Councils, and therefore I am not going past this discussion when we are now discussing this communication in Stortinget. This is a core question. Nordic cooperation and the task of the Nordic Council is therefore to protect these people and to reject all attempts to build moral walls around what we call the Nordic welfare state. So to something quite different: European policies will be characterised next year by a number of changes and shifts: We all know all of the things that relate to brexit. At the end of May elections shall be held in the European Parliament. A new EU Commission shall then also be appointed and approved. Negotiations on the EU’s next seven-year budget, for the period 2021–2027, are already under way. A number of policy choices are to be made and new cabals are to be added. In this context, it makes little sense to talk about a Nordic block in Europe. Nor are Norway and Iceland EU members. We should not overestimate the importance of the Nordic region, but we should not underestimate the importance that Nordic cooperation can potentially play in European cooperation. In particular, I would like to highlight the Nordic labour market model, with tripartite cooperation and other specific features. As Nordic politicians, we should work – as many people say – for the Nordic model to inspire both European institutions and national authorities. The work to highlight the Nordic model must also mean taking seriously and combating the threat to our Nordic working life model, for example. various forms of social dumping and undermining the conditions of workers. Here in Norway we should be able to welcome European workers, but only if it is based on Norwegian pay and working conditions. Finally, in these days it has been exactly 70 years since the negotiations on a separate Nordic defence union started between the governments of Stockholm, Copenhagen and Oslo. The talks started in May 1948, a few months after the coup in Prague and the Communist takeover of power in Czechoslovakia. But the story took, as you know, another way. One year later, in Washington on April 4, 1949, both Denmark, Iceland and Norway joined in establishing the Atlantic Pact. Today, when we are discussing the further development of Norwegian defence cooperation, it is not about turning back at 70 years and realising the plans for a Nordic defence union – of course it does not. Nevertheless, there is still a significant untapped potential for closer Nordic defence and security cooperation. This will be one of the important areas for further development of the Nordic model and Nordic cooperation.

If we look at the Nordic region as a whole, the patient base is much larger, with 25 million, and we have good health records that we can use better. The Nordic Council of Ministers has contributed funds to three sub-projects on ICT solutions, the exchange of health data and clinical studies on medical testing. Social security, prevention and innovation and research organisation are the main areas of Nordic social and health cooperation. As head of the Welfare Committee in the Nordic region for the last two and a half years, I have followed closely on this work, and it is very exciting and very important. In the autumn of 2017, a working group was set up to look at the exchange of information and experience on medicinal product prices and reimbursements. Here's a lot to pick up in a good Nordic collaboration. Antibiotic resistance is one of the major health challenges facing the world today. If we don't get over-consumption of antibiotics, in a few years' time, we'll be able to experience that people are dying of something as common as pneumonia. Norway and the Nordic countries are today international drivers in the fight against antimicrobial resistance. The challenges and measures in this area must be seen in a global context and in a perspective where public health, animal health and the environment are seen in the context of ‘one health’. A Nordic One Health AMR strategy group and an expert group have been established to strengthen Nordic cooperation and develop common Nordic positions in the dialogue with the EU. Here we already see the fruits of Nordic cooperation. Last June, when the European Commissioner for Health and Food Safety presented the new EU Action Plan on Antibiotic Resistance in the European Parliament, he opened with reference to Norway and the Nordic countries – an example of the importance of Nordic cooperation.

The Nordic Council also considered that a Nordic fund could be created to finance investigations, analyses, planning, etc. relating to transport infrastructure and extending across the Nordic borders. It was also a decision on the investigation of bottlenecks and how business and labour markets in the districts can be developed by considering parallel or alternative transport routes. So to the last point, which at least I see as one of the heaviest. It is for the Nordic Council to reach a unanimous request for the restoration of a Council of Transport Ministers. You had that in the past, it was removed. The Nordic Council has discussed this for several times and for a long time, but is now unanimous that the transport area is so important that it is requested to establish a Council of Ministers for Transport. I hope that the Council of State can respond positively to the fact that it wants to do so at least from the Norwegian side, because the Nordic countries – and hence Norway – will continue to be one of the world's leading cooperation regions, must therefore have the possibility of moving goods and people in place. This requires a good overview and coordination. There is the challenge to the Council of State, and it is therefore provided by a unanimous Nordic Council.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e347	Nordic cooperation is very important. We have many common values in the Nordic region as the organized cooperation further develops and strengthens. The organised cooperation between Nordic parliamentarians in the Nordic Council is one of the main reasons for the continued development of Nordic cooperation. The Nordic region has too much of its territory in a common understandable language, which is important to continue. Therefore, it is important that the Nordic languages are used during the collections and that an active interpreter is used where necessary. The Nordic countries have had open borders long before the adoption of the Schengen Agreement. The value of it has been very clear on the North Callot, where people have also traditionally moved freely, regardless of the border that was subsequently drawn. In parts of North Norway, several languages live side by side, and it is equally natural to use Norwegian, Sami, Kvinsk and Finnish. This example illustrates that the boundaries have not been closed to Nordic consonants. As a finnmarking, I feel native to the North Calotte, that this is a region that has dense linguistic, cultural and family ties. The hope is that it should always be like this. The Nordic Council has addressed important issues where we can achieve more through cooperation than each country alone. An example of this is the state of the world’s seas, linked to pollution — in particular plastic — and management of fish stocks. These areas are particularly important for Norway as a port. Norway has a large sea area of over 2 million. km2 that we manage. It provides Norway as a nation with access to natural resources, both renewable and non-renewable. Our fishing resources have led to settlement, employment, export income and economic prosperity along our long coast. The sea is rich, and we as a nation are lucky to have the approach to the sea that we have. Norway manages its fish stocks with the aim of being sustainable and yielding returns each year. Stocks must not be overfished, as in the worst case a stock may collapse. For 2018, our fishing quotas for white fish, i.e. cod, hay and seich, are 600 000 tonnes. Norwegian fisheries exported for over 100 billion. Norwegian Krone in 2017. This means food for the world’s population and income for the fishing industry and the nation of Norway. Norway is working to eliminate the so-called UUU fishing, the illegal, unregulated and unreported fishing. By gaining control of UUU fisheries, managing fish stocks can be sustainable in the long term. Control of UUU fishing would not be possible without Norway investing in marine-related research. The Marine Research Institute in Bergen carries out extensive scientific work related to stock research, stock monitoring, fisheries biology and the general state of the sea. We have many coastal educational environments linked to maritime industries, as well as farming and fishing. Competence in this area in Norway is high and can be transferred to other countries. In order to reap the wealth of the sea from a generational perspective, it is important that world seas are not polluted. Our obligation is to keep our seas clean and not polluted. It is a work that requires both changes in the attitudes of those who are users of the sea and strong efforts by our authorities to regulate and control this area. This has to be done in several areas: we are all concerned about plastic in the sea. We got an eye opener when it stranded a whale that had its stomach full of plastic, not food. We have plastic eyes floating around the oceans, we have microplastics that are absorbed into fish and other living organisms, we have environmental toxins, waste oil emissions and ballast water discharges, to mention some of the challenges. So we have a lot to work with in the Nordic region. But there are also positive things happening, with both changes in attitudes in the population and technological progress. We social democrats are technology optimists, but we are also supporters of regulating to improve the situation. In terms of maritime transport, both of people and of goods, we are facing a technological revolution, and the maritime industry is moving ahead with great innovation. In Norway, we have many ferry routes. The first all-electric ferry section is running on the Anda-Lote route. The ferries have battery packs and infrastructure is developed for both charge current and shore power. The first electric jack is in full operation in the municipality of Karlsøy in Troms. Developments are at record speed, and it's exciting to follow. The battery and powerboat infrastructure is not yet in place and the size of the batteries must also be reduced if they are to be used by all types of boats. In this area, action and innovation are needed. We know we're going to get autonomous, self-driving ships in a short time. In Norway, Trondheimsfjorden is assigned to this test area. Driverless ships shall eliminate human failure and possibly human errors leading to the contamination of vulnerable sea areas. Another related theme is the increased tourist influx of cruise ships in Arctic areas, as well as ice-free north-east passage and major freight traffic. It means greater risk of pollution and greater risk of accidents. Norway has progressed to improve search and rescue in the northern regions. In the event of an accident, it is essential that sufficient capacity is available in this area. A boat accident with a cruise ship or a freight boat in the northern waters may have disastrous consequences for crew and passengers and discharges affecting fish stocks and seabirds. We are so lucky that we are reaping the resources of the sea, so we have obligations to manage these resources so that the sea is still clean. The Workers' Party is pleased that the Nordics are united on these challenges. Finally, as a finnmarking, I am very pleased with the Finnish initiative relating to the construction of the railway from Rovaniemi to Kirkenes. It will strengthen the North Callot, it will give us great opportunities for alternative travel and freight routes and will, as I said, contribute to further strengthening this region.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e446	We are now considering a proposal where the proposers propose that the government should investigate and modify the arrangement for air charges. The aim of this review shall be to ensure that charges strengthen the provision of the Short-Range Network. One now sees that the system of charges leads to a distortion in the distribution of the tax burden. It is such that small aeroplanes with few seats have a significantly higher charge per passenger than large aeroplanes with many seats. In addition, there is a significant difference between short and long routes. There are greater charges on flights to return Stokmarknes-Bodø than, for example, return Oslo-London. Some of the charges are based on weight and other charges on the number of aircraft seats. The consequences are in any case that ticket prices become so high that it weakens the competitive relationship with other modes of transport. Flights on the short-haul network are considered to be public services in large parts of the country. The distances are large, and other travels become heavy and take a long time. It is therefore important that the charges do not result in accidental cuts in the number of flights. The minority in the Committee considers that there is a need to change the fees, so that smaller aeroplanes do not have a higher passenger charge than large aeroplanes — and, where appropriate, that environmental charges are differentiated according to travel distance. By doing so, one will then follow the polluter-pays principle. However, it is also the case that the inhabitants of Northern Norway often depend on travelling far to get short. What do I mean by that? This means that journeys between aerodromes must be made via Oslo. For example, a journey between Kirkenes and Trondheim has to be made via Oslo. A review of the charges must therefore also take account of this relationship. The majority of the Committee opposes this proposal, on the grounds that the government has already started work. However, this proposal goes beyond that, as the proposers will assess the total tax burden and not just the passenger fee. That's why it's strange that the majority won't go in for it. The short-haul network in Norway is created for a coming electric aircraft revolution. Electric aircraft will initially come as relatively small aeroplanes, with relatively short range. They'll fit perfectly on this web. That's why it's also important that we maintain the activity at the airports, this type of aerodromes, for the next ten years. When electric planes take over these flights, there will be a solution to the travel pattern in large parts of the country. They're getting cheaper to fly, and they won't pollute very much. Therefore, the Workers’ Party and the Centre propose that the Stortinget ask the Government to carry out this review of the fees and modify them in a way that meets these challenges. In this way, on behalf of the Workers' Party, I take up the proposal we have made together with the Centre Party in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e1374	I would like to take this opportunity to thank the Committee for its cooperation on the suspension. Oslo and Akershus have a special responsibility as the hub of the transport system both on the road and on the road and in the air. I would like to stress the importance of this, especially as regards Norway's ability to achieve the climate objectives set out in the Paris Agreement. Transport needs are high in this area, and the shift from private cars to public transport, bicycles and corridors and the transfer of goods from road to sea and rail provides a major positive impact on national statistics. Much of the goods transported by road and rail are destined for the Oslo region, and many of the work journeys and leisure trips have their origin or end point here. More than 50 pst. of the country’s collective travel takes place in Oslo and Akershus – a total of 371 million journeys per year. But despite this, there are more than 112 million passages in the bombing in the year – or 306,000 passages per day – one way. Much of this is useful traffic, but daily work travel also accounts for a large proportion. An annual population growth of 15 000 to 20 000 inhabitants per year further increases transport demand. When the committee was traveling in Oslo and Akershus a few weeks ago, it was good to be able to record the offensive attitude to these challenges as a meeting in Oslo and Akershus. The propositions we're dealing with today are based on Prop. 86 S for 2016-2017, which was adopted in Stortinget last year. It proposed the introduction of a new tariff system with time- and environmental-differentiated tariffs at three different stages. Step 1 involved the introduction of time and environment-differentiated toll tariffs in existing toll sections in Oslo. Those amendments were introduced on 1 October 2017. The proposal also set out projects and actions assumed to be funded by Oslopackak 3. Step 2 requires additional bombing at the Oslo city border for traffic from Rome and Follo against Oslo. At the same time, new collection points are planned in Oslo on Ring 2 with arms. Furthermore, it is proposed to introduce a charge for electric cars in tariff group 1. The aim is to introduce step 2 from 1 March 2019. Step 3 further increases the tariffs for electric cars in tariff group 1. It is an objective to introduce stage 3 from 1 March 2020. The local authorities have concluded that the State will contribute 50 pst. of the investment costs of several heavier public transport infrastructure projects. It's the Fornebuban, a new centre tunnel for the T-Bahn, an orbital solution for Lower Rome and a new T-Bahn signalling and securing system. Operation, maintenance and upgrading of existing T-Bahns and overall plan for upgrading existing T-Bahns adapted to new T-Bahns are also part of the locally revised agreement. It is worth noting that several of the major public transport projects considered relevant by local authorities are at an early stage of the plan and investigation. High uncertainty is therefore linked to cost estimates. The further priority shall be managed through portfolio management and the scope and priorities shall be assumed to be adapted to the available financial framework. The Ministry indicates that the loan envelope shall not exceed SEK 6,3 billion. 2013-kronor. The Ministry further stresses that, in the case of possible 50/50 projects, funds have been allocated in the National Transport Plan 2018-2029 to Fornebubanen and new T-Bahn Tunnel between Majorstuen and Tøen/Ensjøen. Of the total tolls, it is estimated that about 72 pst. will go to investment and operation of public transport, 13 pst. for bicycle measures and 15 pst. to clean road measures. It is worth noting that local authorities have agreed that battery-electric vehicles will pay tolls for the introduction of Oslopackak 3 step 2. This means that from 1 March next year, payments will be made in the bombardment for clean electric cars. It is within the national rules adopted by the Storting. One important factor is that many more toll stations are set up that allocate expenditure to several. It will provide lower tariffs for many of those currently running through the bombing process, while increasing overall revenue. Time and environment-differentiated tariffs will also be continued in the new bomb sections.

User payments are a natural part of the funding base for new road projects where there is a local desire to do so. At the same time, we believe that the toll load can clearly reach a pain limit. That is why the Christian People's Party has made a strong contribution to increasing the appropriations for road and transport in recent years, and that effort will continue. It makes sense to take out part of the toll potential that exists, provided that there is local political will to do so. However, in order to maintain the user payment, it is important not to experience the scheme unfairly or very unfairly, including: with regard to geography. In some areas, there are trends in the retention of high-priced toll stations, which may be particularly stressful for some particularly vulnerable groups. It would therefore make sense to look closely at the whole of the user payment system, precisely for the purpose of ensuring the most robust and sustainable system in the future. The total toll burden for road users in an area should also be assessed in the local processes. In particular, it is important that road users experience a good relationship between payment and use. I therefore understand the criticism that is being addressed to the Oslo package when it comes to this particular point. We accept the agreement negotiated in the Oslo package, but at the same time the agreement illustrates the need to think again. The solution is not to introduce 100 pst. State funding. We spend more government money on the road than ever before. The Christian People's Party has been involved in negotiating the most ambitious transport plan in Norway. In addition, NTP has a base of 131 billion. kroner in tolls, and there's a lot. We have done that because in the projects where it is desired, it has been decided locally. And we do it because it contributes to an even more way – in addition to what the state already grants. So, in total, we're going to spend over 1,000 billion. kr for transportation during the planned period, and more than half going on. With 100 pst. State funding must have spent 131 million on the Community. Extra kroner. We have the choice between saying yes to more roads and tolls and saying no to other purposes, such as health, defence, school, etc. We're going to have user payments, but we should renew ourselves. We should take it very seriously, for example. Advanced road pricing can be a more forward-looking system than the current tolling system. Evolution goes in that direction. Today's toll stations will, in my opinion, appear to be old-fashioned and out of date in a few years' time. I think we should just as well get used to the idea that there are other and more fair ways to organise user payments than today's toll system. Road pricing can, in our opinion, be a good and forward-looking system, and I'm sure we'll come back to this in a very short time.

There are also some concerns. In particular, the Olum bombing position can become an unreasonably high load for some of the people who live in this area. The section we are currently dealing with is part of the east–west link E16 Bergen–Gävle. The road serves both as a local road and as a main road link between Gardermoen/Oslo North and Buskerud, connecting to both the South and the West. This is an important outer ring road bet north of Oslo, which will be able to relieve the overburdened road system through Oslo, which we also talk about in the previous case. At the same time, the development project will also ensure that transit traffic is conducted outside the Jevnaker Centre and the neighbouring residential areas. In addition, walking and cycling conditions are improved. The aim of the development is to achieve a satisfactory standard with regard to both road safety, the environment and traffic management. Funding is based on government funds and tolls. A plant start-up is planned during the current year and the project is expected to open up for traffic in 2021. Work on the local road network shall be completed the following year. It's assumed that 80 million. kr of the financing pot shall benefit the existing E16. The governance frame is – estimated at 2018 price level – of SEK 2.7 billion. NOK, with a total cost framework of well 3 billion. kr. The project has been estimated to have a positive impact on the reduction of CO 2 emissions. The reduction in CO 2 emissions is calculated as much as 24 200 tonnes in the opening year 2021. Today's road is narrow and curved, with partly poor geometry and relativity. The road width varies between 6 and 7.5 metres – on parts of the E16 route the only provision for both walking, cycling and running. On the Buskerud border-Olum section, 64 injuries have occurred between 2005 and 2015. The project covers the construction of 12,5 km of two-field road, with a width of 12,5 metres and a speed limit of 90 km/h. The road is built with a mid pulley and sections with a pass-by field in one or both directions. The road will lie in a new path, with the exception of a short one when connecting to the existing one at Olum. The project will provide a high level of traffic security gains. In particular, after the completion of the new E16 and the upgrading of existing E16, there will be an entirely different experience of moving on the route. It is therefore very gratifying to propose a three-point recommendation from a Joint Committee. Now the population of Jevnaker can finally experience plant start-ups — over the course of the current year — for this long-term extension.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e2076	At the request of the Transport and Communications Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 5 minutes to each party group and 5 minutes to members of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e2085	Today we are discussing a proposal for a representative on smart cities. The proposal is to ask the government to develop a national smart city strategy dealing with safe and open smart city infrastructure, including to explore the need to secure national data platforms and standards and to ensure public ownership of data in order to secure the Community's share of future value creation and to provide a basis for future innovation. The Transport and Communications Committee took a preliminary position on the matter on 8 May and sent it to the Municipal and Management Committee for its opinion. As rapporteur, I would like to thank the Committee for its constructive cooperation. A unanimous committee strongly agrees with the proposers that Norway needs a smart city strategy and that the opportunities for innovation, jobs and better cities for its citizens are enormous if properly and timely. For example, investing in smart cities can contribute to better mobility, better urban use, better urban information, more efficient use of energy and other resources, and better urban planning decisions. The Committee would point out that the concept of smart cities has taken root internationally, as a collective term in innovative, ICT-based ways of organising small and large parts of urban life, and that the purpose of smart cities is to renew, simplify and improve cities, from the perspective of citizens, businesses and the public sector. The Committee also agrees that it is important that the municipalities themselves are in the driving position in this work, preferably in interaction with local business, because it is the municipalities themselves who know best what the key areas of local action are. Many municipalities are currently taking good and exciting initiatives, using digital technologies to improve the quality of life of citizens, provide better services and reduce climate and environmental problems. The Committee notes that, to date, the government does not consider it appropriate to develop its own national strategy for smart cities, and points out that both the need and the challenges, as well as the priorities and initiatives of municipalities, are too different for a common national recipe to be appropriate. The Committee further points out that a significant increase in technology and digitisation efforts in the transport sector has been announced through the National Transport Plan. Developments in new technologies are taking place at a high pace, and Norway has one of the world’s most technologically advanced populations and good digital infrastructure, and Norway, as a small country, can move quickly and benefit from close cooperation between public, business and academia. The Jeløya platform shows that the government will carry out a comprehensive evaluation of Norwegian laws and regulations, with a view to identifying unnecessary rules that prevent or delay the use of transport technology and ICT solutions. The use of intelligent traffic systems, autonomous solutions and other technologies in the transport sector shall be facilitated with improved capacity utilisation, increased road safety and improved provision to travellers. The Committee welcomes this. So far there is a great deal of consensus in the committee. The members of the Committee of the Right, the Progressive Party and the Left point out that the Government Platform calls for the strengthening of public sector innovation efforts in order to ensure good services, better coordination and good use of society’s resources. The Jelaua platform is clear that the government will develop a strategy for digitisation in the public sector. The ongoing initiatives, mechanisms and measures already in place, together with the government platform, will contribute to important improvements in order to realise smarter cities and communities. We want to highlight that the government is already working specifically, we are going to follow this subject and continue to work to identify specific obstacles or barriers where cities and municipalities themselves signal that there is a need for increased coordination. The JELUEA platform is also clear that the government will develop a public sector digitalisation strategy to strengthen efforts, cooperation and coordination. The Right, Left and Advance Party therefore believe that the government is already delivering on the punk, and agree that it is not natural for new, specific items to be created on the state budget now. In view of the above, the members of the Committee of the Right, Left and Progressive Party are of the opinion that the proposal for a representative on smart cities should not be adopted. I expect other parties to present their proposals.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e2110	Smart cities are a relatively new concept. ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e2209	The company TaKaYou have developed software and sensors that can analyze a whole town's water supply. Up to half of all water entering the water pipes never reaches the taps, but gets lost in hidden faults and leaks. Then TaKaYou can calculate up to where the major leakages occur, and better water use, make cheaper repairs and give a smoother pressure. The company Nordic Automation Systems has developed smart street lights with sensors that turn street lights on and off depending on needs, pedestrians, cyclists, etc. There are also companies with air quality sensors that can provide the public with real-time information on air quality in different urban areas, which is particularly important for those with asthma or other respiratory diseases. There are solutions and companies that allow electric cars to sell power back from batteries to the grid and through smart meters to help stabilise the grid, e.g. in case of a voltage drop. Sensors are available for parking spaces, public transport solutions, general traffic flow monitoring and traffickers. Today, there are sensors and computer systems to digitise all infrastructure, such as water, roads, air, electricity and light – all that makes cities good and safe to live in. So all this physical infrastructure is about to get a digital layer on top of it – sometimes called the Internet of Things. Together, these major data from all systems become a new digital publicity, a common resource, a generalisation that we all use and contribute to. This enables smarter solutions and better living in practice for citizens, while this digitisation can improve resource flow and eliminate waste. But today, we have no national strategy or standard to ensure a general public access to these major data, our digital generalisations. Many traffic data are e.g. Google as owner, and then Google decides how to access them. It seems obvious that there should be open standards, which ensure equal access to the public – open standards for better competition, democratic technology development and transparency. In this context, it is strange that the majority of the Committee refers to ‘needs, challenges and local authorities’ priorities and initiatives being too different for a common national recipe to be appropriate’. This is weird. ‘National strategy’ has been replaced by the word ‘prescription’ and the majority’s rejection of the proposal makes no sense. It's inconsistent with what else they say they're doing in the setting. The result is that since the government's digital agenda was launched, little has happened in the smart city area, and Norway is sinking downwards on the international smart city rankings. To this end, instruments should be set up to promote cooperation between cities, coordination and work sharing of best practice, so as to avoid duplication of work, or to create each one’s system. National instruments should therefore launch projects promoting labour-sharing and cooperation between municipalities and cities, and we need to stimulate work on open standards, high data security and public access based on the urban communities. They have this in common. But these good instruments contained in our proposals are about to be put to the vote, it seems. It is obviously more important for the parties in the position to reject and vote on constructive proposals – not because they are bad, but because they may come from the opposition. Reference is made in the timing and debate to a lot of different initiatives with something smart or digital in them: NTP, self-propelled vehicle, public digitisation, the existence of support schemes that actors can apply for. All of this that's mentioned is well and good, but it's not related, and it shows a lack of strategic understanding of what smart cities are, and what a strategic bet is. These initiatives do not go to the heart, namely the use of digital publics for better life and higher value creation with lower footprints. This is weak parliamentary work. I am a little disappointed when constructive cross-cutting proposals are to be rejected by unwarranted, polarised, party-locked dividing lines. We're facing an in-depth paradigm shift that's far more important than the party divisions. IT was previously something inside the computers, on a screen, but soon it will be all around us. This will be the basis for the functioning of modern cities. So it's sad that the proposals are voted down – for us in Norway, for the environment, for business and perhaps for the world as well.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e2330	The government has therefore put forward a proposal to amend the electoral law so that in 2021 the Grand elections can be conducted with today's 19 electoral districts. Predictable frameworks around the election implementation are very important and such issues are resolved well in advance of the elections. Predictability is important both for voters, for lots and for electoral authorities. The division into electoral districts by decision-making is a very central part of our electoral system, and I believe it is important that this be thoroughly investigated and discussed before making any lasting changes. The question was now examined by the Committee on Election Law, where all parts of the Stortinget were represented. I note that the Committee believes that it is a good solution for the electoral law to facilitate the application of the 19 electoral districts until a new division, if any, has been thoroughly reviewed. As the Committee says, this solution is supported by a large majority of the hearing bodies. Today's division into electoral districts takes account of geographical and party political representation, and it would be appropriate and predictable to keep it while waiting for the inquiry into the Election Law Committee. The investigation shall be completed at the beginning of 2020 and will then be sent to a wide-ranging consultation before promoting a proposal for a new electoral law and any changes in the electoral districts of the Grand Duchy. We have also proposed a change in terms of who has the right to sign on a list proposal. It follows from the current rules that a list proposal of sufficient size shall be signed by at least two of the members of the board of the party's local division in the county or municipality to which the list applies. With mergers of municipalities and county councils, there is a need to make this provision a little more flexible than it is today. The electoral law should not provide guidance on how the parties choose to organise themselves in the new entities. It is therefore proposed to amend this in such a way that a list proposal is to be signed by two members of the local unit responsible for the electoral district to which the list applies. For the batches it is important to have clarity about who can sign, in good time before the nomination processes start. The proposition presented today also includes an evaluation of the 2017 elections. The general impression is that the choice was made in a good and reliable way. One of the most important follow-up points is how we can maintain safety and trust in future choices. As is well known, the department laid down a pre-election rule last year which required all municipalities to make a preliminary manual counting of ballots. This change was implemented in a good way in all municipalities and did not lead to delays in counting. The evaluation also shows that this change led to calming down the question of the safety of the enumeration solution. I believe it is essential that our voters have confidence and understanding of how the choice is being made. This trust is created through transparency and information. The question about whether the option is safe is likely to arise again before the next choice. The Ministry is now reviewing all the evaluation reports, and we will also consider how best to ensure this in future elections. In this context, of course, it is also important that the Storting helps to create predictability in the electoral process. By adopting the amendments to the electoral law, we avoid creating uncertainty about the rules of the game in our democracy. Otherwise, the evaluations show that the electronic system used for selection is highly valued by the users, who are our municipalities and county municipalities. The elections in 2017 were the first elections in which municipalities could freely choose to cross the number of people in electronic form on election day, and a whole of 255 municipalities chose to make use of this scheme.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e2372	Since the 1990s, it has been a goal that freight transport over long distances should be transferred from road to sea and track as far as possible. National Transport Plan 2014-2023 states that the government will strengthen and facilitate the competitiveness of maritime and rail freight. Through international agreements, Norway has for many years committed itself to climate change reduction. Nevertheless, CO 2 emissions from road freight transport are increasing. Compared to the population, Norway had about 35 pst. more killings in heavy-duty hatches than average in Europe. The aim of the National Audit Office's investigation has been to assess developments in the transport of goods by road, sea and rail where there is competition between modes of transport, and the reasons for the possible lack of transport of goods by road and rail. In this report, the national audit shows that the objective of transporting freight by sea and by rail has not been achieved. This means that, in the years 2010-2015, for the carriage of goods on routes over 300 km close to port or rail terminal, road transport has increased as much or more than maritime and rail transport. And if the road transport on these routes had been carried out on sea and track instead, the cost of society as a result of accidents, pollution, noise, road wear and cows would have been monthly less. The report also states that the Governing Boards have not been able to strengthen competition in maritime and rail transport compared to road transport, and it is shown that the Ministry has had weak follow-up to the freight transfer objective and that one has not had enough management information. Neither the Department nor the ETAs, according to the National Audit Office's assessment, have had any information on the extent to which the actions taken have really been effective. Lack of information and knowledge may have resulted in actions that could involve the transfer of goods not having been proposed or implemented. Finally, the report points out that the Ministry has taken action to achieve the objective of getting more freight from road to sea and rail. The National Audit Service recommends that the Ministry of Transport provides for a common strategy to pursue the agreed objective of transport of goods by sea and rail, that economic instruments be strengthened, that planned measures be implemented and that new measures be implemented, in order to increase the competitiveness of sea and rail compared to road. In addition, the action page points out that freight transport needs to be targeted specifically at land-use planning processes around ports and rail terminals, in such a way that it also facilitates the transport of goods from sea to rail. The National Audit Office is also clear in its recommendation that the Ministry should ensure stronger control and follow-up of the effects of freight transport measures and the achievement of the objective of transferring goods from road to sea. There is a joint control and constitution committee that essentially adheres to the Riksrevisions and which is also behind the comments on the case. But the government party on the Right and the Progress Party are united on additional comments, which I assume representatives of these parties themselves will do a little bit more to do.

This will also make it easier to measure the results of the measures taken. In the latest state budgets and in the national transport plan 2018-2029, measures have been put in place that will contribute positively to freight transport, and the Minister for Transport has facilitated a better management system. Therefore, the freight analysis, developed in the context of the national transport plan 2018-2029, provided valuable information on the freight flows and not least a mapping of the transmission potential. Therefore, the objective of freight transmission set out in the National Transport Plan 2018-2029, adopted by the Stortinget, is better anchored in good analysis and should therefore also be more realistic to achieve. With the measures already implemented and those contained in the National Transport Plan 2018-2029, I have good faith that it will be possible to achieve the objective shared by the Stortinge to transfer 30 pst. of the long sea and rail road transport operations.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e2651	I would first like to thank the members of the Committee for their cooperation on this matter. The aim of the study has been to assess the extent to which Nav manages labour market measures in a targeted way and in line with the Storting decision and assumptions. The survey covers the period 2010-2016. The Supervisory and Constitutional Committee considers it appropriate and important that the Riksrevision examines the use of labour market measures in Nav. It is absolutely necessary to ask questions about the proper functioning of the measures. The National Audit Office has identified several issues that need to be corrected and the Committee is concerned about the effective functioning of labour market measures. Storing and government shall facilitate a good, inclusive and decent working life, in which all those who so wish shall be able to participate. Nav is very central to the task of getting this done. Active participation in the labour market can be crucial for the quality of life, well-being and belonging to individuals — yes, absolutely crucial. Work allows individuals to use their resources, knowledge and competences. It gives a social arena, a place to belong, and not least work is important to prevent poverty. It is an overall objective of labour and welfare policies to increase employment and reduce social security. Those who lose their jobs need to move quickly to new jobs, while at the same time strengthening the efforts of those who have been outside for a long time. We spend a lot of resources on labour market measures. So the Riksrevision partially reveals significant weaknesses in the administration. The main findings can be summarised in this way: Many action participants do not come into work after they have completed labour market measures. Nav demonstrates to varying degrees whether the labour market measure is necessary and appropriate. A large proportion of users do not have an activity plan at the start of actions, which is a right under the Nav law. There is a lack of follow-up of action participants from the Nav offices, and there are weaknesses in government management and reporting of the use of labour market measures. The activity plan is intended to be a work tool to ensure that the Nav office is targeted and that the follow-up is adapted to the individual user. The Riksrevision believes that it is highly critical that such a central instrument has almost only been developed exceptionally in the period 2010–2016. An overall committee fully agrees with that criticism. We ask the department to follow the case closely to make sure that the law is complied with and the standard is complied with. The National Audit Office considers that there is a lack of follow-up from the Nav offices to the action participants. The average waiting time for people with disabilities prior to a measure is between six and eleven months for the most used labour market measures. The Committee agrees with the Riksrevision that the lack of follow-up here is critical. Early efforts are essential to succeed in getting people to work. The Committee considers it critical that a large number of users do not receive follow-up as foreseen. We now call for an evaluation. The outsourcing of labour market measures should be evaluated in order to assess the results achieved and the changes in the tendering practices put in place to improve the achievement of the objectives. I expect the ministry to follow up on that an overall committee here asks for. Lack of governance and a weak focus on the quality of labour market measures and the achievement of results in the way the Riksrevision covers is, in the Committee's view, critical. We spend a lot of resources on this, but the most important thing is, of course, that mistakes and shortcomings affect individuals when the measures are not good enough. There's got to be improvements here. I refer you to the setting, where the Right and Advance Party, on the one hand, and the Workers' Party, the Centre Party and the SV, on the other hand, have some own comments, but all in all, the Committee is united in its criticism. We believe that the lack of governance and the weak focus on the quality and performance of labour market measures are critical. There is also a joint committee behind the recommendations of the National Audit Office. So it's my hope that this study provides the basis for learning and improvement at all levels.

[[...]] My research into the practice of the scheme in relation to adults with mental retardation shows that the interests of the protection authorities are largely ahead of the interests of the individual people in the scheme.’ Those are scary words. All people have the right to decide about their own lives. The researcher, who has analysed 167 defence cases from one county office, shows that the rule of self-determination is the rule rather than the exception. These interventions are also not justified. The contracting authorities shall ensure that the interests of the person under protection are safeguarded. However, the researcher shows that the individual’s interests are rarely defined on the basis of his or her own wishes or personality. There are no standards to determine the individual’s own decision-making competence. It often depends on medical statements that appear quite random. When a reader of the National Audit Office's report on the way the corporate reform has been carried out, with this researcher's description in mind, it is striking how much they are. The transfer of protection measures from municipality to state would provide increased legal certainty, increased degree of individualisation and flexibility, while the Riksrevision states that there are shortcomings in the adaptation of protection to the needs of the person seeking protection, lack of training and supervision, lack of national guidelines, lack of control and variable follow-up, and lack of legal certainty. Often the forger is his or her own family, usually his or her parents. Sometimes the load is so heavy that they can't take any more, and they ask for a new warden to be appointed. I have even been contacted by parents who have experienced that the local authority and the local authority have suddenly decided that they should move from owner-occupied housing to municipal housing, have lost the right to a user-controlled personal assistant, are refused to go on a planned holiday with the family, etc. The parents do not have the right to complain in such cases. The majority in the control committee shows, for example: to the NFU, which says that current practices threaten both legal certainty and human rights. We can't have it like that -- as Leirstein quite correctly said. The Labour Party has put forward proposals in a case on the rights of people with disabilities where we ask the government to bring about the necessary legislative changes. After the Riksrevision report, that proposal should receive a majority when it is discussed here in the Chamber on 12 June. I urge the Council of State to ask its party traps to vote for that proposal, on the basis of the statement he himself stood here and kept.

Of those we support, I would like to highlight in particular the scheme of minority counsellors in school, the measures linked to the risk of children being left abroad against their will, and the clarification of parental responsibility to prevent children from being subjected to sexual mutilation. Where we have not included in the proposals, the reason is often that the government has informed that they are already implementing similar measures or investigations. There are also a number of measures we need more information about to support, and will therefore await government information. In particular, it concerns proposals to prohibit sibling childhood. I look forward to a good debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e3162	The underlying reason for what we are discussing here today, social control, physical and mental honour in different immigrant environments, is that we have brought old traditions and rules from the caste and clan communities into our modern democracy. In the caste and clan communities, large families choose which family they plan to connect with through the marriage of children, because it can have a significant impact on the family’s economy, status, power, etc., as their societies are organized. through marriage and later inheritance. These fundamental positions or rules are in stark contrast to our modern democracy and welfare societies, where the freedom, equality and the right to make their own free choices are fundamental. So it's a very big question that we're discussing at Stortinget when we deal with these things. I attended and arranged a meeting at the Furuset in mid-February, where we saw how this affects young people today. Surprisingly, many people, approx. 300, who was very engaged. They told gripping stories about how they experienced social control, how they felt that it was planned to whom they were going to marry, and how it is impossible, among other things. to have other layings in the environments in which they live. It has great consequences for the whole life cycle. That is why we have made 22 proposals, covering everything from common rules, national rules on common swimming lessons, participation in camp schools – i.e. growing-up conditions – to issues such as: certificates of integrity and non-obligational checks relating to marriage. I am sure that Stortinget, the representatives here, understand that these are urgent and important questions. The young people gathered at the Furuset in mid-February, the 300, have seen that debates have been going on for 20–30 years, for example. on sibling childhood, without it being concluded, but that one is going to investigate it further. Of course, it's different quality, it's different imams teaching at the Qur'an Schools, but some of the children are subjected to quite strong indoctrination in attitudes that are in the direction of what we usually call Islamist, where we talk about the unfaithful societies, and where we create divisions in society from early childhood, and at least many children are subjected to sex segregation, etc. So the question of the supervision of Qur'an schools, which we're dealing with in proposal No. 2, and which has the support of the Workers' Party and the Swedish Labour Party, is something I would like to be further thinking about how we should get started better, and get on with it. Another proposal, which concerns represals, restrictions, how to enforce social control, for which unfortunately we have not received a majority, applies to children who are left behind, often in the country of origin of the family. When the Institute of Social Research examined and produced a report in 2014 on how many children were left abroad, the number in their research report, which I have not seen in doubt, was 3,285 children, approximately. 1,450 children in Somalia. In particular, Pakistan, Iraq, Afghanistan and Somalia are the countries with the most abandoned children. Last year, 61 children were reported to the U.D., i.e. to U.D. integration advisers and embassies abroad. The gap between 61 and 3285 in the Institute of Social Research report is important for the Storting to be aware of. I don't want to say that all of the 3,285 are having a hard time, or that some of them are not going to good schools, but there is also reason to believe that quite a few of them are not doing that, and are not getting proper training, which parents have a duty to ensure that they get under the training law. I'm worried that we can't go into it any more, because you often use terms like those that are left abroad 'against their will', but it's clear that children don't talk so that parents can be subjected to child protection measures, police, authorities, etc., so it's a lot for the kids to report this, even if they're left behind. I have even met children on the T-Bahn who have told me that they have been left behind for two to three years, without having been registered anywhere. So we have proposed a reporting system, so that we get a better overview of how many are left behind, and so that the embassies can check and make sure that they actually go to good schools, and get hold of more cases than the 61 they were told about. We believe that this is a very serious question, when we look at the figures that the last report actually showed. We have also proposed that the Child Protection Act be amended so that child protection can take advantage of their possibilities, including: through families, to address issues that occur in countries that have not signed the Hague Convention, where Norwegian child protection has several rights to operate. For example, those who have not signed the Hague Convention are: Somalia and Pakistan. ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e3285	Negative social control and honor-related violence are about oppressive structures that prevent young girls and boys from being what they want, and love what they want. It's all about everything from not being able to go to a party and meet that boy a like, to extreme control and violence in the form of being tricked out of the country and being held by relatives against their will. These repressive structures apply to all cultures, at different times, in different ways and to different degrees. It affects children born and raised in Norway with parents from another country, it affects members of conservative Christian environments, and it can take the form of the negative social control of minority people by the majority population. What's going on in all kinds of negative social control is that it's an outrage to people's freedom to be themselves. Therefore, the fight against negative social control is a universal liberation struggle. It's a natural continuation of the women's struggle we've waged in this country for a hundred years. And precisely because this is about extensive structures, we in SV have been busy listening to those who are involved, and to those who are working with and against this, when we have promoted our proposals. The message we have been given has been quite clear: what is needed is concrete and constructive proposals that prevent social control and strengthen the provision of help to the brave boys and girls who break control and who view this problem as part of the problem of violence in close relations. Therefore, SV has chosen to propose 21 concrete measures to combat negative social control. While some parties have held seminars at Stortinget and other parties have again competed for the strictest possible rhetoric and the toughest symbol proposals, we have taken action over words and promoted proposals requested by the victims of social control and by those working against it. We have proposed preventive measures such as extending the system of minority advisors and having probationary systems with our own minority advisors in kindergartens and kindergartens. We have proposed to extend the system of integration advisers at the embassies to help those who are being taken out of the country. We have proposed to strengthen the assistance for those who break out, by legislating the right to assistance in all municipalities, so that your address does not determine what kind of help you get. We have proposed to strengthen the housing and support offer, so that you have a safe place to go if you are going to break up with the family. But obviously you can't always take action over words. The Government will not support binding and concrete decisions on more prevention and strengthening assistance for those who break out. SV proposes 21 measures, and it is clear that 17 of them have not been taken into account in the government’s work. This could lead to the double discrimination of many minority women, both on the basis of their gender and on the basis of their ethnicity. This scheme also proposes to strengthen the government in the revised national budget, with 13 new advisers. We also have a team of experts to assist, and the government has taken a number of measures to strengthen the competence of the ordinary services that meet with children, young people and adults who are exposed to this. Although we have a foreign service and competent integration advisors at the embassies, the opportunity to assist is considerably more limited when the person in need of help is abroad. It is therefore essential to prevent departure if we suspect that children or young people may be subjected to coercion or abuse. I have now taken the initiative to review cases in violent and criminal proceedings before the police, prosecution authorities and courts. Both in the interests of the individual child and in the interests of clarification of the case, it is important that the investigation should start rapidly. There has been a marked increase in the number of organised child questionings, especially by vulnerable children. The proportion of organised interrogations carried out under the statutory deadline has gradually improved, but many children are still waiting for too long. The State Children's House has been considerably strengthened since 2013. From 1 September 2017, responsibility for the medical examinations at children’s homes is added to regional health establishments. The social pediatric competence of health businesses has been strengthened, and all children questioned at the State Children’s Home are offered medical examinations. Children and young people are not only exposed to violence and abuse from adults but also from peers. Often the abuses are committed by their own siblings, friends, and girlfriends. The Ministry of Health and Welfare has given Health West the task of directing work to strengthen competence and establish treatment provision in all health regions for children and young people with problematic or harmful sexual behaviour. In order to prevent relapse, the treatment of offenders under criminal law is also provided. The basic project targets sexual offenders in three Norwegian prisons. I want to put in place a treatment offer that helps to ensure that pedophiles do not reintroduce themselves after settlement. Prevention can't start early enough. Themes related to body and borders are an essential part of the training cycle in kindergarten and school. In the new framework plan for kindergartens, this is included in the subject area Body, movement, food and health. In the post-renewal of primary education, the theme Public Health and Lifelong Learning will address issues related to respect for the borders of others. A number of measures are now being implemented through the escalation plan to enhance the competence of the service provider. Developments include: a common national strategy of competence against violence and abuse. Cooperation between services can be challenging, and in particular the different rules on professional secrecy, information rights and disclosure obligations. They may impede the exchange of information. I have started more work to raise this issue. The administrative law committee is one. In addition, the Storting has adopted new provisions on child protection disclosure. An investigation of the different rules on professional secrecy, information rights and disclosure obligations is also on the steps. In my opinion, no further work should be started in this area. The proposals of the representatives will require extensive investigations and, to a large extent, tantamount to ongoing or planned work. Regarding the proposal to revise and clarify Section 25 of the Health Personnel Act, I have been informed by the Minister of Health and Health that the main challenge is not lack of legal access to information between the partners, but that the employees in the department do not always know or understand the legislation. Knowledge raising should therefore have priority. The abolition obligation of the penal code § 196 gives a strong argument to go further with confidentiality-related information. Nevertheless, there is much evidence that many more cases where children are exposed to violence should have been reported to the emergency services. We will now consider in more detail how to ensure a broad knowledge and understanding of the elimination obligation by either amending the provisions or otherwise. Sound cross-border cooperation is essential in order to provide good assistance to vulnerable persons and to detect violence and abuse. Important cooperation models are the State Children's House, the new support centres for the vulnerable and Project November at the Stovner Police Station. As regards the proposal for transparency of the parties, I have been informed by the Minister for Children and Gender Equality that the issue of the transparency of the parties and the obligation to provide information in matters relating to child protection will be considered in the context of a new child protection law. The rules on exemptions from the access to child protection are detailed in a new 2017 circular on child protection proceedings.

The hearing sent by the Ministry of Justice and Defence is concerned with a proposal to impose a fee on the issue by the courts of replacement and replacement certificates and on the issue by the courts of powers to dispose of less valuable dwellings. The consultation note refers to a proposal to introduce such a fee from 1 July 2018 and the fee is addressed in the Ministry of Justice and Defence's budget proposal for 2018. The essence of the matter is that if the spouse, partner or parent dies, the heirs must have a replacement certificate in order to be able to transfer the values of the deceased to themselves. This applies to real estate as a dwelling. So far it has been free for individuals to get such a certificate, but now the government will charge a fee on this service. The government proposes that it will cost people who have recently lost one of their closest, $1150 to get the certificate. This totals 40 million. kr per year. In the State Council’s letter of reply to the Committee of 9 April 2018, the Council of State reasonably states that a fee corresponding to the expenditure incurred by the public in such acts of authority is paid and that the use of resources in such issues is not insignificant. Furthermore, the Council of State points to a comparable fee and does not see decisive arguments for the imposition of the fees referred to in the proposed representative rather than those. The Council of State further points out to the 2017 Perspective Communication that the scope of action will be more limited in the coming years and that consideration should be given to how Norwegian services should be financed in the future. ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e4315	This case concerns an SV representative proposal, where the proposers originally asked the Government to ask the government to complete the work of the selection to assess changes in emergency legislation. The proposal has its background in the fact that on 9 February this year the government will set up a public selection to assess changes in emergency legislation. The selection is mentioned in the Report. St. 10 for 2016–2017, Risk in a safe society, which was addressed in the previous ageing period. From the government's side, it is shown that there may be a need to act faster and more flexibly from the authorities' side in order to deal with critical situations in a better way than today's laws and regulations allow. The selection has been given a mandate to consider whether the government should have the possibility of supplementing or making exemptions from legislation and of temporarily suspending or modifying the statutory courts in exceptional situations. The suggestion providers refer to Inn comments. 326 S for 2016-2017, cf. Report. St. 10 for 2016-2017, Risk in a Secure Society, where the majority in the forthcoming Judicial Committee encouraged the government not to set up a selection to assess an extension of the emergency legislation’s scope of action. This call was not made as a suggestion, based on the fact that she did not have the majority in Stortinget behind her. The action was standing in the message, and that is the point where the government is now following up. The Committee, through its examination of this matter, has largely recognised the government's right to set up selections and the duty it has to follow up several decisions taken by the Stortinget. The SV's proposal has been drawn up in the Committee's deliberations, and I therefore propose a new proposal from the Committee's pluralities, from the Labour Party, the Centre Party and the Socialist Left. The proposal now before us is primarily about restricting the mandate of the selection. Legislation in exceptional situations of crisis may address challenges of a different nature. Situations may be such that within the current legal framework there is no access to the necessary measures, with the consequence that it will not be possible to deal with a critical situation in a timely manner. Such situations can result in such a large and rapid pressure on resources that it is not possible to meet the obligations that depend on the authorities. The authorities will then be in a situation where the only way to deal with the crisis is to set aside the normal procedures. I find it strange that the proposers would like to conclude on such an important matter before the decision is taken. When the proposers argue against changes in the law on preparedness, they give the main reason that the need has not been adequately justified by the government. The right-wing party and its representatives in the Committee will stress that the selection has just been mandated to identify and document the need for an extension of regulation beyond what exists today. On the one hand, the proposers demand proof of the need, but on the other hand, they do not want the need to be met. In the mandate of the selection there is also an assessment of any objection to such an extended mandate and an explanation of mechanisms that must be in place to prevent abuse. It is right and important that this distribution is carried out at a time of calm and not when one is in the midst of a crisis. Through the selection's professional work, we will receive thorough and thought-out assessments, and we will be informed of several aspects of the matter. There is no selection to conclude in a specific direction. Neither has the government pre-defined this matter in any way, but it wants us to assess whether we have the governance mechanisms we need to deal effectively with an extraordinary crisis situation in the best possible way. Since the relationship with human rights has been discussed in both the note and in the media, I would point out clearly in the mandate of the selection that the content of the distribution and any conclusions and decisions can never conflict with the Constitution or international conventions on human rights. I take up the minority's proposal, which is pending.
