ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e4369	§ 3 of today's emergency law is a concreteisation of constitutional emergency law that only applies in the event of extremely serious incidents. It is only when the kingdom is at war, is threatened by war, or there are serious threats to the security and independence of the kingdom, that the government may deviate from the law, or itself make provisions with law. The government now wants to assess an extension of both the scope and the threshold of this law, and they have set a much debated sample to assess and provide the government with cross-sectoral powers that will give them much lower thresholds to override the decision of the Storting in cases not covered by the Emergency Act. I understand the desire that quick solutions will be provided when difficult situations arise, but the central party believes that the government has not documented or demonstrated the need for the government to be able to make decisions with legal content or disregard the laws of Stortinget, in the cases they have mentioned. I also understand the idea of wanting to think across sectors, but I believe that in this case the government imposes on itself far too wide powers at the expense of the Stortinget. There are certain constraints that the government has to respect. One would like to say that this is a difficult debate in the field of emergency response, which must be taken when we are not in the midst of a crisis. But I cannot see that this proposal will improve preparedness and at the same time be within what should be the relationship of power between magnification and government. I have noticed in particular that the government parties – to justify extended powers to themselves – refer to the asylum situation in 2015 and the pressure that arose on the administration and welfare services at the time. There were no shortcomings in the law that were the major problem then, but that the government did not understand seriously early enough and did not take action when they should have done so. I find it difficult to see good reasons for the Storting's handling at that time to justify the government's power to side with the Storting's law decision. The size showed action and great willingness to cooperate. Experience has shown that it is fully possible to make rapid and sensible decisions in the Grand Duchy when a distressed situation arises. It is difficult to understand that this should be a good reason to change the part of the law currently used only in war, if war threatens, or when there are serious threats to the security and independence of the kingdom, so that the government in practice is given powers far beyond what it currently has. There is a substantial difference between the ordinary regulatory competence of the Stortinget giving the government and a general cross-sectoral mandate that the government wants. The space for action of the executive will be much larger, at the expense of the Grand Government, the principle of legality and, in particular, the citizens. A broad provision of power is particularly problematic when it comes to interfering with the rights of individuals. It should not be up to the government at all times to determine which rights individuals should have or to disregard what the Storing has adopted in this area. The risk of abuse of a broad mandate is high, especially in a matter such as asylum, which is easily politicised. Fear and political pressure can lead to unfortunate decisions that go drastically beyond the rights of the individual, without democratic treatment in the Storting. It is a fundamental principle of democracy that the legal status of individuals is determined by law by the Stortinget, not by regulations based on open powers. The size must be very careful to leave too much of the decisions in this field to the government and the administration alone, and thus open the way for cutting down the individual's rights. The fact that the sample is to stay within the Constitution and the obligations of international law should really be lacking, so I see no reason why the government can boast that this is included in the mandate. However, although a possible proposal remains within the framework of the Constitution and obligations under international law, it would nevertheless badly harmonize with the principle of legality. The Government has already received clear signals from both the current and the previous majority of the Judicial Committee that changes to the Emergency Act are not desirable. The composition of the Committee has changed since the election, but the majority of the Committee remains committed to the message that there is no request for proposals in line with the mandate of the Preparatory Committee. When all the parties, the majority of the Judicial Committee and the majority of the Grand Duchy have expressed themselves as clearly as has been done, the government does not have the majority to change the law on preparedness in line with what they outline, and those proposals should not be promoted.
ParlaMint-NO_2018-05-29.ana.ud123e4394	Signatures have taken the initiative to do so. It happened because I was quite surprised – quite startled in fact – that the government would put down a sample that would look at the possibility of having an extended state of emergency, in other words, waiving existing laws and rights. In the mandate, there is a clear reference to the refugee crisis in 2015. My criticism of the sample also needs to be seen in the context of the fact that the person who took the initiative in the sample was a minister, a minister of justice, who did not respect Norway's international rights in a refugee situation. She wanted Norway to look at opportunities to deviate from its international obligations through a flow of refugees. It was very clear, she says it explicitly, in Dagbladet on 24 August 2017, where she is clear that the European Convention on Human Rights stands in the way of how Norway is to deal with the flow of refugees. As I read this, the Minister of Justice at that time wanted to look for an opportunity for Norway to derogate from the rights of asylum seekers, possibly shut out or prevent asylum seekers from coming to the country. It's with the political background that I suggested that this sample should not work any further. Norwegian preparedness law – which is therefore in line with Article 15 of the European Convention on Human Rights – states that exceptions to the applicable law and law can only be made in the event of war, when war threatens, or when fundamental security is at risk. It therefore requires very strong reasons for extending these proxy and waiver laws. It is not as the representative of the Progress Party says, that there are many things that can trigger an exceptional state. No, that's not it. International conventions are very clear that it is at war, when war threatens, or when other types of fundamental security are at risk, that there may be grounds for an exceptional state. The definition of a flow of refugees as a state of emergency is likely to be in violation of the European Convention on Human Rights, very weakly justified by international law and also a poorly hidden attempt to try new opportunities to limit the flow of asylum to Norway. The previous Minister has thus taken the initiative of a committee with a mandate that is likely to be contrary to Article 15 of the ECHR. That's pretty special. I can also remind the Council of Europe that the use of state-of-the-art in various countries, especially in countries such as Turkey and France, where coup d'états and terrorist acts have been behind. The Council of Europe Parliamentary Assembly adopted a resolution in April, now in spring, which stated very clearly that it is only these three elements, war, when war threatens, or when fundamental security is at risk – only these three things – that can provide the basis for declaring a state of emergency. The Council of Europe strongly stated that a refugee stream is absolutely not the basis for declaring an exceptional state. The fact that this sample has now been given the opportunity to see whether it can be such that a flow of refugees can be the basis for an exceptional state is also contrary to the new Council of Europe resolution. SV originally proposed that the entire sample be dropped, because we believe it was unheard of putting down a sample with these kinds of objectives, but also because the Size has previously expressed that such a sample is undesirable. But we wanted to pool support, and there have now been support from three parties for a joint proposal from the Workers' Party asking the government not to come back to the Stortinge with extended powers. So we are not now proposing that the selection should be dropped, but a strong restriction on the mandate. If it is adopted, the sample will have little to do, and they will not be able to come back to Stortinget with any suggestions. I am a little surprised that we have not received support from the Christian People's Party for this. I think it's eye-catching. It must be the responsibility of the Christian People's Party. By not supporting this proposal, the Christian People's Party is behind a minister who is looking for an opportunity to use waiver laws in a wave of refugees. It's pretty strong. Then they must also take responsibility for the possibility that waiver laws will affect refugees from war zones.

There are strict demands when this court intervenes – only when the kingdom is at war, when war threatens or the independence or security of the kingdom is at risk.
